scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Left-_and_right-hand_traffic" title="Left- and right-hand traffic">Driving side</a></th><td class="infobox-data">right</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Telephone_numbers_in_Liechtenstein" title="Telephone numbers in Liechtenstein">Calling code</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Telephone_numbers_in_Liechtenstein" title="Telephone numbers in Liechtenstein">+423</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/ISO_3166" title="ISO 3166">ISO 3166 code</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/ISO_3166-2:LI" title="ISO 3166-2:LI">LI</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Country_code_top-level_domain" title="Country code top-level domain">Internet TLD</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/.li" title=".li">.li</a></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Liechtenstein</b> (<small>German:&#32;</small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="de-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Standard_German" title="Help:IPA/Standard German">[ˈlɪçtn̩ʃtaɪn]</a></span>&#32;<span data-nosnippet="" id="ooui-php-1" class="ext-phonos ext-phonos-PhonosButton noexcerpt ext-phonos-PhonosButton-emptylabel oo-ui-widget oo-ui-widget-enabled oo-ui-buttonElement oo-ui-buttonElement-frameless oo-ui-iconElement oo-ui-buttonWidget" data-ooui="{&quot;_&quot;:&quot;mw.Phonos.PhonosButton&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;\/\/upload.wikimedia.org\/wikipedia\/commons\/transcoded\/a\/ae\/De-Liechtenstein.ogg\/De-Liechtenstein.ogg.mp3&quot;,&quot;rel&quot;:[&quot;nofollow&quot;],&quot;framed&quot;:false,&quot;icon&quot;:&quot;volumeUp&quot;,&quot;data&quot;:{&quot;ipa&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;text&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;lang&quot;:&quot;en&quot;,&quot;wikibase&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;file&quot;:&quot;De-Liechtenstein.ogg&quot;},&quot;classes&quot;:[&quot;ext-phonos&quot;,&quot;ext-phonos-PhonosButton&quot;,&quot;noexcerpt&quot;,&quot;ext-phonos-PhonosButton-emptylabel&quot;]}"><a role="button" tabindex="0" href="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/a/ae/De-Liechtenstein.ogg/De-Liechtenstein.ogg.mp3" rel="nofollow" aria-label="Play audio" class="oo-ui-buttonElement-button"><span class="oo-ui-iconElement-icon oo-ui-icon-volumeUp"></span><span class="oo-ui-labelElement-label"></span><span class="oo-ui-indicatorElement-indicator oo-ui-indicatorElement-noIndicator"></span></a></span><sup class="ext-phonos-attribution noexcerpt"><a href="/wiki/File:De-Liechtenstein.ogg" title="File:De-Liechtenstein.ogg">i</a></sup>), officially the <b>Principality of Liechtenstein</b> (German: <i lang="de">Fürstentum Liechtenstein</i>),<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> is a <a href="/wiki/German_language" title="German language">German-speaking</a> and <a href="/wiki/Double-Landlocked" class="mw-redirect" title="Double-Landlocked">doubly landlocked</a> <a href="/wiki/Landlocked_country" title="Landlocked country">country</a> and <a href="/wiki/Microstate" title="Microstate">microstate</a> located in the <a href="/wiki/Alps" title="Alps">Alps</a> between <a href="/wiki/Austria" title="Austria">Austria</a> and <a href="/wiki/Switzerland" title="Switzerland">Switzerland</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> It is the sixth smallest nation worldwide.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
Liechtenstein is a <a href="/wiki/Semi-constitutional_monarchy" class="mw-redirect" title="Semi-constitutional monarchy">semi-constitutional monarchy</a> headed by the <a href="/wiki/Prince_of_Liechtenstein" class="mw-redirect" title="Prince of Liechtenstein">prince of Liechtenstein</a>.
</p><p>Liechtenstein is bordered by Switzerland to the west and south and Austria to the east and north. It is <a href="/wiki/List_of_European_countries_by_area" title="List of European countries by area">Europe's fourth-smallest country</a>, with an area of just over 160 square kilometres (62 square miles) and a population of 38,749 (as of 2019<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>).<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Divided into <a href="/wiki/Municipalities_of_Liechtenstein" title="Municipalities of Liechtenstein">11 municipalities</a>, its capital is <a href="/wiki/Vaduz" title="Vaduz">Vaduz</a>, and its largest municipality is <a href="/wiki/Schaan" title="Schaan">Schaan</a>. It is also the smallest country to border two countries.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Economically, Liechtenstein has one of the <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_(PPP)_per_capita" title="List of countries by GDP (PPP) per capita">highest gross domestic products per person in the world</a> when adjusted for <a href="/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity" title="Purchasing power parity">purchasing power parity</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> The country has a strong <a href="/wiki/Financial_services" title="Financial services">financial sector</a> centred in Vaduz. It was once known as a billionaire <a href="/wiki/Tax_haven" title="Tax haven">tax haven</a>, but is no longer on any official blacklists of uncooperative tax haven countries. An <a href="/wiki/Alpine_states" title="Alpine states">Alpine country</a>, Liechtenstein is mountainous, making it a <a href="/wiki/Winter_sport" class="mw-redirect" title="Winter sport">winter sport</a> destination.
</p><p>Liechtenstein is a member of the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations" title="United Nations">United Nations</a>, the <a href="/wiki/European_Free_Trade_Association" title="European Free Trade Association">European Free Trade Association</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Europe" title="Council of Europe">Council of Europe</a>. It is not a member of the <a href="/wiki/European_Union" title="European Union">European Union</a>, but it participates in both the <a href="/wiki/Schengen_Area" title="Schengen Area">Schengen Area</a> and the <a href="/wiki/European_Economic_Area" title="European Economic Area">European Economic Area</a>. It has a <a href="/wiki/Customs_union" title="Customs union">customs union</a> and a <a href="/wiki/Monetary_union" class="mw-redirect" title="Monetary union">monetary union</a> with Switzerland.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="History">History</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: History">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/History_of_Liechtenstein" title="History of Liechtenstein">History of Liechtenstein</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_history">Early history</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Early history">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:2008-05-19_Balzers_Liechtenstein_5472.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/2008-05-19_Balzers_Liechtenstein_5472.jpg/220px-2008-05-19_Balzers_Liechtenstein_5472.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/2008-05-19_Balzers_Liechtenstein_5472.jpg/330px-2008-05-19_Balzers_Liechtenstein_5472.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/2008-05-19_Balzers_Liechtenstein_5472.jpg/440px-2008-05-19_Balzers_Liechtenstein_5472.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3008" data-file-height="2000" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Gutenberg_Castle" title="Gutenberg Castle">Gutenberg Castle</a>, Balzers, Liechtenstein</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Schlossvaduz.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/84/Schlossvaduz.jpg/220px-Schlossvaduz.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/84/Schlossvaduz.jpg/330px-Schlossvaduz.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/84/Schlossvaduz.jpg/440px-Schlossvaduz.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1023" data-file-height="680" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Vaduz_Castle" title="Vaduz Castle">Vaduz Castle</a>, overlooking the capital, is home to the <a href="/wiki/Hans-Adam_II,_Prince_of_Liechtenstein" title="Hans-Adam II, Prince of Liechtenstein">Prince of Liechtenstein.</a></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Johann_Josef_I_von_Liechtenstein.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Johann_Josef_I_von_Liechtenstein.jpg/170px-Johann_Josef_I_von_Liechtenstein.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="214" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Johann_Josef_I_von_Liechtenstein.jpg/255px-Johann_Josef_I_von_Liechtenstein.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Johann_Josef_I_von_Liechtenstein.jpg/340px-Johann_Josef_I_von_Liechtenstein.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2183" data-file-height="2750" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Johann_I_Joseph,_Prince_of_Liechtenstein" title="Johann I Joseph, Prince of Liechtenstein">Johann I Joseph, Prince of Liechtenstein</a> from 1805 to 1806 and 1814 to 1836, by <a href="/wiki/Johann_Baptist_von_Lampi_the_Elder" title="Johann Baptist von Lampi the Elder">Johann Baptist von Lampi the Elder</a>. <a href="/wiki/Liechtenstein_Museum" title="Liechtenstein Museum">Liechtenstein Museum</a>, Vienna</figcaption></figure>
<p>The oldest traces of human existence in the area of present-day Liechtenstein date back to the <a href="/wiki/Middle_Paleolithic" title="Middle Paleolithic">Middle Paleolithic</a> era.<sup id="cite_ref-Early_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Early-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Neolithic" title="Neolithic">Neolithic</a> farming settlements appeared in the valleys around 5300 BCE.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Hallstatt_culture" title="Hallstatt culture">Hallstatt</a> and <a href="/wiki/La_T%C3%A8ne_culture" title="La Tène culture">La Tène cultures</a> flourished during the late <a href="/wiki/Iron_Age" title="Iron Age">Iron Age</a>, from around 450 BCE—possibly under some influence of both the <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Greek" title="Ancient Greek">Greek</a> and <a href="/wiki/Etruscan_civilization" title="Etruscan civilization">Etruscan</a> civilisations. One of the most important tribal groups in the Alpine region were the <a href="/wiki/Helvetii" title="Helvetii">Helvetii</a>. In 58 BCE, at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Bibracte" title="Battle of Bibracte">Battle of Bibracte</a>, <a href="/wiki/Julius_Caesar" title="Julius Caesar">Julius Caesar</a> defeated the Alpine tribes, thereby bringing the region under <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Rome" title="Ancient Rome">Roman</a> subjugation. By 15 BCE, <a href="/wiki/Tiberius" title="Tiberius">Tiberius</a>—later the second Roman emperor—with his brother, <a href="/wiki/Nero_Claudius_Drusus" title="Nero Claudius Drusus">Drusus</a>, conquered the entire Alpine area. Liechtenstein then became integrated into the <a href="/wiki/Roman_province" title="Roman province">Roman province</a> of <a href="/wiki/Raetia" title="Raetia">Raetia</a>. The area was garrisoned by the <a href="/wiki/Imperial_Roman_army" title="Imperial Roman army">Roman army</a>, which maintained large legionary camps at <a href="/wiki/Bregenz" title="Bregenz">Brigantium</a> (Austria), near <a href="/wiki/Lake_Constance" title="Lake Constance">Lake Constance</a>, and at <a href="/wiki/Maienfeld" title="Maienfeld">Magia</a> (Switzerland). The Romans built and maintained a <a href="/wiki/Roman_road" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman road">road</a> which ran through the territory. Around 260 CE Brigantium was destroyed by the <a href="/wiki/Alemanni" title="Alemanni">Alemanni</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Germanic_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Germanic people">Germanic people</a> who later settled in the area around 450.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/Early_Middle_Ages" title="Early Middle Ages">Early Middle Ages</a>, the Alemanni settled the eastern <a href="/wiki/Swiss_plateau" class="mw-redirect" title="Swiss plateau">Swiss plateau</a> by the 5th century and the <a href="/wiki/Valleys_of_the_Alps" class="mw-redirect" title="Valleys of the Alps">valleys of the Alps</a> by the end of the 8th century, with Liechtenstein located at the eastern edge of <a href="/wiki/Alamannia" title="Alamannia">Alamannia</a>. In the 6th century the entire region became part of the <a href="/wiki/Frankish_Empire" class="mw-redirect" title="Frankish Empire">Frankish Empire</a> following <a href="/wiki/Clovis_I" title="Clovis I">Clovis&#160;I</a>'s victory over the Alemanni at <a href="/wiki/Tolbiac" class="mw-redirect" title="Tolbiac">Tolbiac</a> in 504.<sup id="cite_ref-Nationsencyclopedia_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Nationsencyclopedia-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Nationsonline_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Nationsonline-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The area that later became Liechtenstein remained under Frankish hegemony (<a href="/wiki/Merovingians" class="mw-redirect" title="Merovingians">Merovingian</a> and <a href="/wiki/Carolingian_Empire" title="Carolingian Empire">Carolingian</a> dynasties) until the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Verdun" title="Treaty of Verdun">Treaty of Verdun</a> divided the Carolingian empire in 843, following the death of <a href="/wiki/Charlemagne" title="Charlemagne">Charlemagne</a> in 814.<sup id="cite_ref-Early_17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Early-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> The territory of present-day Liechtenstein formed part of <a href="/wiki/East_Francia" title="East Francia">East Francia</a>. It would later be reunified with <a href="/wiki/Middle_Francia" title="Middle Francia">Middle Francia</a> under the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Roman_Empire" title="Holy Roman Empire">Holy Roman Empire</a>, around 1000.<sup id="cite_ref-Early_17-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Early-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Until about 1100, the predominant language of the area was <a href="/wiki/Romansh_language" title="Romansh language">Romansch</a>, but thereafter <a href="/wiki/German_language" title="German language">German</a> began to gain ground in the territory. In 1300 another Alemannic population—the <a href="/wiki/Walser" class="mw-redirect" title="Walser">Walsers</a>, who originated in <a href="/wiki/Valais" title="Valais">Valais</a>—entered the region and settled; the mountain village of <a href="/wiki/Triesenberg" title="Triesenberg">Triesenberg</a> today preserves features of the <a href="/wiki/Walser_German" title="Walser German">Walser dialect</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foundation_of_a_dynasty">Foundation of a dynasty</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Foundation of a dynasty">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>By 1200, dominions across the Alpine plateau were controlled by the Houses of <a href="/wiki/House_of_Savoy" title="House of Savoy">Savoy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Z%C3%A4hringer" class="mw-redirect" title="Zähringer">Zähringer</a>, <a href="/wiki/Habsburg" class="mw-redirect" title="Habsburg">Habsburg</a>, and <a href="/wiki/House_of_Kyburg" class="mw-redirect" title="House of Kyburg">Kyburg</a>. Other regions were accorded the <a href="/wiki/Imperial_immediacy" title="Imperial immediacy">Imperial immediacy</a> that granted the empire direct control over the mountain passes. When the Kyburg dynasty fell in 1264, the Habsburgs under <a href="/wiki/Rudolph_I_of_Habsburg" class="mw-redirect" title="Rudolph I of Habsburg">King Rudolph I</a> (Holy Roman Emperor in 1273) extended their territory to the eastern Alpine plateau that included the territory of Liechtenstein.<sup id="cite_ref-Nationsencyclopedia_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Nationsencyclopedia-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> This region was <a href="/wiki/Feoffment" title="Feoffment">enfeoffed</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Hohenems" title="Hohenems">Counts of Hohenems</a> until the sale to the <a href="/wiki/Liechtenstein_dynasty" class="mw-redirect" title="Liechtenstein dynasty">Liechtenstein dynasty</a> in 1699.
</p><p>In 1396 <a href="/wiki/Vaduz" title="Vaduz">Vaduz</a> (the southern region of Liechtenstein) gained <a href="/wiki/Imperial_immediacy" title="Imperial immediacy">imperial immediacy</a>, i.e. it became subject to the Holy Roman Emperor alone.<sup id="cite_ref-Eccardt_2005_176_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Eccardt_2005_176-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The family from which the principality takes its name originally came from <a href="/wiki/Liechtenstein_Castle" class="mw-redirect" title="Liechtenstein Castle">Liechtenstein Castle</a> in Lower Austria, which they had possessed from at least 1140 until the 13th century (and again from 1807 onwards). The Liechtensteins acquired land, predominantly in <a href="/wiki/Moravia" title="Moravia">Moravia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lower_Austria" title="Lower Austria">Lower Austria</a>, <a href="/wiki/Silesia" title="Silesia">Silesia</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Duchy_of_Styria" title="Duchy of Styria">Styria</a>. As these territories were all held in <a href="/wiki/Fee_(feudal_tenure)" class="mw-redirect" title="Fee (feudal tenure)">feudal tenure</a> from more senior feudal lords, particularly various branches of the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Habsburg" title="House of Habsburg">Habsburgs</a>, the Liechtenstein dynasty was unable to meet a primary requirement to qualify for a seat in the Imperial diet (parliament), the <span title="German-language text"><i lang="de"><a href="/wiki/Imperial_Diet_(Holy_Roman_Empire)" title="Imperial Diet (Holy Roman Empire)">Reichstag</a></i></span>. Even though several Liechtenstein princes served several Habsburg rulers as close advisers, without any territory held directly from the Imperial throne, they held little power in the Holy Roman Empire.
</p><p>For this reason, the family sought to acquire lands that would be classed as <span title="German-language text"><i lang="de">unmittelbar</i></span>, or held without any intermediate feudal tenure, directly from the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Holy Roman Emperor">Holy Roman Emperor</a>. During the early 17th century <a href="/wiki/Karl_I,_Prince_of_Liechtenstein" title="Karl I, Prince of Liechtenstein">Karl I of Liechtenstein</a> was made a <span title="German-language text"><i lang="de">Fürst</i></span> (prince) by the Holy Roman Emperor <a href="/wiki/Matthias,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Matthias, Holy Roman Emperor">Matthias</a> after siding with him in a political battle. <a href="/wiki/Hans-Adam_I,_Prince_of_Liechtenstein" title="Hans-Adam I, Prince of Liechtenstein">Hans-Adam I</a> was allowed to purchase the minuscule <a href="/wiki/Lordship_of_Schellenberg" title="Lordship of Schellenberg"><span title="German-language text"><i lang="de">Herrschaft</i></span> ('Lordship') of Schellenberg</a> and the <a href="/wiki/County_of_Vaduz" title="County of Vaduz">county of Vaduz</a> (in 1699 and 1712 respectively) from the Hohenems. Tiny Schellenberg and Vaduz had exactly the political status required: no feudal lord other than their <a href="/wiki/Count" title="Count">comital</a> sovereign and the <a href="/wiki/Suzerainty" title="Suzerainty">suzerain</a> Emperor.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Principality">Principality</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Principality">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Herzogshut_Liechtenstein_(1).JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1a/Herzogshut_Liechtenstein_%281%29.JPG/220px-Herzogshut_Liechtenstein_%281%29.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1a/Herzogshut_Liechtenstein_%281%29.JPG/330px-Herzogshut_Liechtenstein_%281%29.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1a/Herzogshut_Liechtenstein_%281%29.JPG/440px-Herzogshut_Liechtenstein_%281%29.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2304" data-file-height="1728" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Ducal_hat_of_Liechtenstein" title="Ducal hat of Liechtenstein">Ducal hat of Liechtenstein</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>On 23 January 1719,<sup id="cite_ref-creation_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-creation-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> after the lands had been purchased, <a href="/wiki/Charles_VI,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Charles VI, Holy Roman Emperor">Charles VI, Holy Roman Emperor</a>, decreed that Vaduz and Schellenberg were united and elevated the newly formed territory to the dignity of <span title="German-language text"><i lang="de">Fürstentum</i></span> ('<a href="/wiki/Principality" title="Principality">principality</a>') with the name "Liechtenstein" in honour of "[his] true servant, <a href="/wiki/Anton_Florian,_Prince_of_Liechtenstein" title="Anton Florian, Prince of Liechtenstein">Anton Florian of Liechtenstein</a>". On this date, Liechtenstein became a sovereign member state of the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Roman_Empire" title="Holy Roman Empire">Holy Roman Empire</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>By the early 19th century, as a result of the <a href="/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars" title="Napoleonic Wars">Napoleonic Wars</a> in Europe, the Holy Roman Empire came under the effective control of France, following the crushing defeat at <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Austerlitz" title="Battle of Austerlitz">Austerlitz</a> by <a href="/wiki/Napoleon" title="Napoleon">Napoleon</a> in 1805. In 1806 Emperor <a href="/wiki/Francis_II,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Francis II, Holy Roman Emperor">Francis II</a> abdicated and <a href="/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Holy_Roman_Empire" title="Dissolution of the Holy Roman Empire">dissolved the Holy Roman Empire</a>, ending more than 960 years of feudal government. Napoleon reorganized much of the Empire into the <a href="/wiki/Confederation_of_the_Rhine" title="Confederation of the Rhine">Confederation of the Rhine</a>. This political restructuring had broad consequences for Liechtenstein: the historical imperial, legal, and political institutions had been dissolved. The state ceased to owe an obligation to any feudal lord beyond its borders.<sup id="cite_ref-creation_23-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-creation-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Modern publications generally attribute Liechtenstein's sovereignty to these events. Its prince ceased to owe an obligation to any <a href="/wiki/Suzerainty" title="Suzerainty">suzerain</a>. From 25 July 1806, when the <a href="/wiki/Confederation_of_the_Rhine" title="Confederation of the Rhine">Confederation of the Rhine</a> was founded, the Prince of Liechtenstein was a member, in fact, a vassal, of its <a href="/wiki/Hegemon" class="mw-redirect" title="Hegemon">hegemon</a>, styled <i>protector</i>, the French Emperor Napoleon I, until the dissolution of the confederation on 19 October 1813.
</p><p>Soon afterward, Liechtenstein joined the <a href="/wiki/German_Confederation" title="German Confederation">German Confederation</a> (20 June 1815&#160;&#8211;&#32;23 August 1866), which was presided over by the <a href="/wiki/Emperor_of_Austria" title="Emperor of Austria">Emperor of Austria</a>.
</p><p>In 1818, <a href="/wiki/Johann_I_Joseph,_Prince_of_Liechtenstein" title="Johann I Joseph, Prince of Liechtenstein">Prince Johann I</a> granted the territory a limited constitution. In that same year <a href="/wiki/Aloys_II,_Prince_of_Liechtenstein" title="Aloys II, Prince of Liechtenstein">Prince Aloys</a> became the first member of the House of Liechtenstein to set foot in the principality that bore their name. The next visit would not occur until 1842.
</p><p>Developments during the 19th century included:
</p>
<ul><li>1842: the first factory for making ceramics was opened.</li>
<li>1861: the Savings and Loans Bank was founded along with the first cotton-weaving mill.</li>
<li>1866: the German Confederation was dissolved.</li>
<li>1868: the Liechtenstein Army was disbanded for financial reasons.</li>
<li>1872: a railway line between Switzerland and the Austro-Hungarian Empire was constructed through Liechtenstein.</li>
<li>1886: two bridges over the Rhine to Switzerland were built.</li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="20th_century">20th century</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: 20th century">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Until the end of <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a>, Liechtenstein was closely tied first to the <a href="/wiki/Austrian_Empire" title="Austrian Empire">Austrian Empire</a> and later to <a href="/wiki/Austria-Hungary" title="Austria-Hungary">Austria-Hungary</a>; the ruling princes continued to derive much of their wealth from estates in the Habsburg territories, and spent much of their time at their two palaces in Vienna. <a href="/wiki/Johann_II,_Prince_of_Liechtenstein" title="Johann II, Prince of Liechtenstein">Johann II</a> appointed <a href="/wiki/Carl_von_In_der_Maur" title="Carl von In der Maur">Carl von In der Maur</a>, an Austrian aristocrat, to serve as the <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Liechtenstein" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime Minister of Liechtenstein">Governor of Liechtenstein</a>. The economic devastation caused by World War I forced the country to conclude a <a href="/wiki/Liechtenstein%E2%80%93Switzerland_relations#Cooperation" title="Liechtenstein–Switzerland relations">customs and monetary union</a> with its other neighbour <a href="/wiki/Switzerland" title="Switzerland">Switzerland</a>.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Franz_I_von_Liechtenstein_in_Russia.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/00/Franz_I_von_Liechtenstein_in_Russia.jpg/170px-Franz_I_von_Liechtenstein_in_Russia.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="233" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/00/Franz_I_von_Liechtenstein_in_Russia.jpg/255px-Franz_I_von_Liechtenstein_in_Russia.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/00/Franz_I_von_Liechtenstein_in_Russia.jpg/340px-Franz_I_von_Liechtenstein_in_Russia.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1357" data-file-height="1858" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Franz_I,_Prince_of_Liechtenstein" title="Franz I, Prince of Liechtenstein">Franz I, Prince of Liechtenstein</a> from 1929 to 1938</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1929, 75-year-old <a href="/wiki/Franz_I,_Prince_of_Liechtenstein" title="Franz I, Prince of Liechtenstein">Prince Franz I</a> succeeded to the throne. He had just married <a href="/wiki/Elisabeth_von_Gutmann" title="Elisabeth von Gutmann">Elisabeth von Gutmann</a>, a wealthy woman from Vienna whose father was a Jewish businessman from Moravia. Although Liechtenstein had no official Nazi party, a Nazi sympathy movement arose within its National Union party. Local Liechtenstein Nazis identified Elisabeth as their Jewish "problem".<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In March 1938, just after the <a href="/wiki/Anschluss" title="Anschluss">annexation of Austria by Nazi Germany</a>, Franz named as regent his 31-year-old grandnephew and heir-presumptive, <a href="/wiki/Franz_Joseph_II,_Prince_of_Liechtenstein" title="Franz Joseph II, Prince of Liechtenstein">Prince Franz Joseph</a>. Franz died in July that year, and Franz Joseph succeeded to the throne. Franz Joseph II moved to Liechtenstein in 1938, a few days after Austria's annexation.<sup id="cite_ref-Eccardt_2005_176_22-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Eccardt_2005_176-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During <a href="/wiki/World_War_II" title="World War II">World War II</a>, Liechtenstein remained officially neutral, looking to neighbouring Switzerland for assistance and guidance, while family treasures from dynastic lands and possessions in <a href="/wiki/Bohemia" title="Bohemia">Bohemia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Moravia" title="Moravia">Moravia</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Silesia" title="Silesia">Silesia</a> were taken to Liechtenstein for safekeeping. At the close of the conflict, <a href="/wiki/Czechoslovakia" title="Czechoslovakia">Czechoslovakia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Poland" title="Poland">Poland</a>, acting to seize what they considered German possessions, expropriated all of the Liechtenstein dynasty's properties in those three regions. The expropriations (subject to <a href="/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Liechtenstein#International_dispute_with_Czechoslovakia,_Czech_Republic_and_Slovakia" title="Foreign relations of Liechtenstein">modern legal dispute</a> at the <a href="/wiki/International_Court_of_Justice" title="International Court of Justice">International Court of Justice</a>) included over 1,600&#160;km<sup>2</sup> (618&#160;sq&#160;mi) of agricultural and forest land (most notably the UNESCO listed <a href="/wiki/Lednice%E2%80%93Valtice_Cultural_Landscape" title="Lednice–Valtice Cultural Landscape">Lednice–Valtice Cultural Landscape</a>), and several family castles and palaces.
</p><p>In 2005, it was revealed that Jewish slave labourers from the <a href="/wiki/Strasshof_an_der_Nordbahn" title="Strasshof an der Nordbahn">Strasshof</a> <a href="/wiki/Concentration_camp" class="mw-redirect" title="Concentration camp">concentration camp</a>, provided by the <a href="/wiki/Schutzstaffel" title="Schutzstaffel">SS</a>, had worked on estates in Austria owned by Liechtenstein's Princely House.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Citizens of Liechtenstein were forbidden to enter Czechoslovakia during the <a href="/wiki/Cold_War" title="Cold War">Cold War</a>. The diplomatic conflict revolving around the controversial postwar <a href="/wiki/Bene%C5%A1_decrees" title="Beneš decrees">Beneš decrees</a> resulted in Liechtenstein not having international relations with the <a href="/wiki/Czech_Republic" title="Czech Republic">Czech Republic</a> or <a href="/wiki/Slovakia" title="Slovakia">Slovakia</a>. Diplomatic relations were established between Liechtenstein and the Czech Republic on 13 July 2009,<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> and with Slovakia on 9 December 2009.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 20 September 1990, Liechtenstein was admitted into the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations" title="United Nations">United Nations</a> as 160th member state. As a member of the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_General_Assembly" title="United Nations General Assembly">United Nations General Assembly</a>, the microstate is one of the few not to play a prominent role in <a href="/wiki/List_of_specialized_agencies_of_the_United_Nations" title="List of specialized agencies of the United Nations">UN-specialized agencies</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Financial_centre">Financial centre</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Financial centre">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Liechtenstein was in dire financial straits following the end of World War II. The Liechtenstein dynasty often resorted to selling family artistic treasures,<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> including the portrait <i><a href="/wiki/Ginevra_de%27_Benci" title="Ginevra de&#39; Benci">Ginevra de' Benci</a></i> by <a href="/wiki/Leonardo_da_Vinci" title="Leonardo da Vinci">Leonardo da Vinci</a>, which was purchased by the <a href="/wiki/National_Gallery_of_Art" title="National Gallery of Art">National Gallery of Art</a> of the United States in 1967 for <span style="white-space: nowrap"><a href="/wiki/United_States_dollar" title="United States dollar">US$</a></span>5&#160;million ($44 million in 2022 dollars),<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> then a record price for a painting.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By the late 1970s, Liechtenstein used its low <a href="/wiki/Corporate_tax" title="Corporate tax">corporate tax</a> rates to draw many companies and became one of the wealthiest countries in the world.
</p><p>Liechtenstein is one of the few countries in Europe (along with <a href="/wiki/Monaco" title="Monaco">Monaco</a> and <a href="/wiki/San_Marino" title="San Marino">San Marino</a>) <a href="/wiki/Tax_haven" title="Tax haven">not to have a tax treaty</a> with the United States, and efforts towards one seem to have stalled.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As of September&#160;2019<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup> the <a href="/wiki/Prince_of_Liechtenstein" class="mw-redirect" title="Prince of Liechtenstein">Prince of Liechtenstein</a> is the world's sixth <a href="/wiki/List_of_the_richest_royals" class="mw-redirect" title="List of the richest royals">wealthiest monarch</a>, with an estimated wealth of <span style="white-space: nowrap">US$</span>3.5&#160;billion.<sup id="cite_ref-CEOWorld2019_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CEOWorld2019-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> The country's population enjoys one of the <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_(PPP)_per_capita" title="List of countries by GDP (PPP) per capita">world's highest standards of living</a>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Government">Government</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Government">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Politics_of_Liechtenstein" title="Politics of Liechtenstein">Politics of Liechtenstein</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Liechtenstein_-_Gemeinden_mit_Exklaven.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/Liechtenstein_-_Gemeinden_mit_Exklaven.png/260px-Liechtenstein_-_Gemeinden_mit_Exklaven.png" decoding="async" width="260" height="422" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/Liechtenstein_-_Gemeinden_mit_Exklaven.png/390px-Liechtenstein_-_Gemeinden_mit_Exklaven.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/Liechtenstein_-_Gemeinden_mit_Exklaven.png/520px-Liechtenstein_-_Gemeinden_mit_Exklaven.png 2x" data-file-width="1359" data-file-height="2206" /></a><figcaption>Administrative divisions of Liechtenstein, showing numerous exclaves</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Landtagsgeb%C3%A4ude_und_Regierungsgeb%C3%A4ude.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/Landtagsgeb%C3%A4ude_und_Regierungsgeb%C3%A4ude.jpg/220px-Landtagsgeb%C3%A4ude_und_Regierungsgeb%C3%A4ude.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/Landtagsgeb%C3%A4ude_und_Regierungsgeb%C3%A4ude.jpg/330px-Landtagsgeb%C3%A4ude_und_Regierungsgeb%C3%A4ude.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/Landtagsgeb%C3%A4ude_und_Regierungsgeb%C3%A4ude.jpg/440px-Landtagsgeb%C3%A4ude_und_Regierungsgeb%C3%A4ude.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3543" data-file-height="2357" /></a><figcaption>The centre of government in <a href="/wiki/Vaduz" title="Vaduz">Vaduz</a></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Hans-Adam_Prince_of_Liechtenstein_(1974)_by_Erling_Mandelmann.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/Hans-Adam_Prince_of_Liechtenstein_%281974%29_by_Erling_Mandelmann.jpg/170px-Hans-Adam_Prince_of_Liechtenstein_%281974%29_by_Erling_Mandelmann.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="254" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/e/e8/Hans-Adam_Prince_of_Liechtenstein_%281974%29_by_Erling_Mandelmann.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="201" data-file-height="300" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Hans-Adam_II,_Prince_of_Liechtenstein" title="Hans-Adam II, Prince of Liechtenstein">Hans-Adam II, Prince of Liechtenstein</a>, as pictured by <a href="/wiki/Erling_Mandelmann" title="Erling Mandelmann">Erling Mandelmann</a> in 1974</figcaption></figure>
<p>Liechtenstein has a somewhat enigmatic political system, which combines elements of <a href="/wiki/Monarch" title="Monarch">absolute monarchy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Representative_democracy" title="Representative democracy">representative democracy</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Direct_democracy" title="Direct democracy">direct democracy</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Monarchy_of_Liechtenstein" title="Monarchy of Liechtenstein">monarch</a> retains extensive executive and legislative powers, and plays a strong active role in the day to day politics of the country, and over all three <a href="/wiki/Branches_of_government" class="mw-redirect" title="Branches of government">branches of government</a>. Representative democracy and direct democracy coexist in that elected parliament enacts <a href="/wiki/Legislation" title="Legislation">legislation</a>, and also voters can propose and enact laws and constitutional amendments independently of the legislature<sup id="cite_ref-Marxer_2007_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Marxer_2007-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>. However, as with laws passed by the legislature, these can be vetoed by the monarch.  
</p><p>The reigning Prince is the Head of State and represents Liechtenstein in its international relations (although Switzerland has taken responsibility for much of Liechtenstein's diplomatic relations).<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The current <a href="/wiki/Constitution_of_Liechtenstein" title="Constitution of Liechtenstein">Constitution of Liechtenstein</a> was <a href="/wiki/Liechtenstein_constitutional_referendum,_2003" class="mw-redirect" title="Liechtenstein constitutional referendum, 2003">adopted in March 2003</a>, amending the 1921 constitution, giving the prince extensive veto powers, and the ability to dismiss the government and rule by emergency decree, and maintaining the prince's active role in the legislative process. The <a href="/wiki/BBC" title="BBC">BBC</a> characterizes Liechtenstein post-2003 as "in effect" an <a href="/wiki/Absolute_monarchy" title="Absolute monarchy">"absolute monarchy</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Legislative authority is vested in the unicameral <a href="/wiki/Landtag_of_Liechtenstein" title="Landtag of Liechtenstein">Landtag</a>, made up of 25 members elected for maximum four-year terms according to a proportional representation formula. Fifteen members are elected from the <a href="/wiki/Oberland_(electoral_district)" title="Oberland (electoral district)">Oberland</a> (Upper Country or region) and ten from the <a href="/wiki/Unterland_(electoral_district)" title="Unterland (electoral district)">Unterland</a> (Lower Country or region).<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Parties must receive at least 8% of the national vote to win seats in parliament, i.e., enough for two seats in the 25-seat legislature. Parliament proposes and approves a government, which the Prince formally appoints. Parliament may also pass votes of no confidence in the entire government or individual members.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Government" title="Government">government</a> comprises the head of government (<a href="/wiki/Prime_minister" title="Prime minister">prime minister</a>) and four government councillors (ministers), who are appointed by the Prince upon the proposal of parliament and with its concurrence, and reflect the balance of parties in parliament. The constitution stipulates that at least two government members be chosen from each of the two regions.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> The members of the government are collectively and individually responsible to parliament; parliament may ask the Prince to remove an individual minister or the entire government, or the Prince may do so unilaterally.
</p><p>Parliament elects from among its members a "Landesausschuss" (National Committee) made up of the president of the parliament and four additional members. The National Committee is charged with performing functions of parliamentary supervision. Parliament shares the authority to propose new legislation with the Prince, and with the citizenry, as both parliament and the citizenry may initiate referendums.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability"><span title="The material near this tag failed verification of its source citation(s). (September 2023)">failed verification</span></a></i>&#93;</sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Dates_and_numbers#Chronological_items" title="Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Dates and numbers"><span title="archive of Lichtenstein&#39;s government webpage, and not the right part of it for the claim. Probably better to find a few source, maybe dig through https&#58;//www.regierung.li/default.aspx?lang=en (September 2023)">needs update</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/The_courts_of_Liechtenstein" title="The courts of Liechtenstein">Judicial authority</a> is vested in the Regional Court at Vaduz, the Princely High Court of Appeal at Vaduz, the Princely Supreme Court, the Administrative Court, and the State Court. The State Court rules on the conformity of laws with the constitution and has five members elected by parliament<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (September 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>.
</p><p>On 1 July 1984, Liechtenstein became the last country in <a href="/wiki/Europe" title="Europe">Europe</a> to grant women the right to vote. The <a href="/wiki/Liechtenstein_women%27s_suffrage_referendum,_1984" class="mw-redirect" title="Liechtenstein women&#39;s suffrage referendum, 1984">referendum on women's suffrage</a>, in which only men were allowed to participate, passed with 51.3% in favour.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="International_awards">International awards</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: International awards">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 2013, Liechtenstein won for the first time a SolarSuperState Prize in the Solar category recognizing the achieved level of the usage of photovoltaics per population within the state territory.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> The SolarSuperState Association justified this prize with the cumulative installed photovoltaic power of some 290 watts per capita at the end of 2012. This placed Liechtenstein second in the world after Germany. Also in 2014, the SolarSuperState Association awarded the second place SolarSuperState Prize in the Solar category to Liechtenstein.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> In the years 2015 and 2016, Liechtenstein was honoured with the first place SolarSuperState Prize in the Solar category because it had the world's biggest cumulative installed photovoltaic power output per capita.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Maltese_Consulate,_Schaan_(1Y7A2240).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Maltese_Consulate%2C_Schaan_%281Y7A2240%29.jpg/170px-Maltese_Consulate%2C_Schaan_%281Y7A2240%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="212" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Maltese_Consulate%2C_Schaan_%281Y7A2240%29.jpg/255px-Maltese_Consulate%2C_Schaan_%281Y7A2240%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Maltese_Consulate%2C_Schaan_%281Y7A2240%29.jpg/340px-Maltese_Consulate%2C_Schaan_%281Y7A2240%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3790" data-file-height="4737" /></a><figcaption>Maltese consulate in <a href="/wiki/Schaan" title="Schaan">Schaan</a></figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_relations">Foreign relations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Foreign relations">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Liechtenstein" title="Foreign relations of Liechtenstein">Foreign relations of Liechtenstein</a></div>
<p>In the absence of political or military power, Liechtenstein has sought to preserve its <a href="/wiki/Sovereignty" title="Sovereignty">sovereignty</a> over the past 200 years through membership in legal communities. <a href="/wiki/Multilateralism" title="Multilateralism">International cooperation</a> and European integration are therefore constants of Liechtenstein's foreign policy, aimed at continuing to safeguard the country's sovereignty as recognized under international law. Decisive for the domestic legitimacy and sustainability of this foreign policy were and are strong direct-democratic and citizen-oriented decision-making mechanisms, which are anchored in Liechtenstein in the Constitution of 1921.
</p><p>Important historical stages in Liechtenstein's integration and cooperation policy were its accession to the <a href="/wiki/Confederation_of_the_Rhine" title="Confederation of the Rhine">Confederation of the Rhine</a> in 1806,<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> to the German Confederation in 1815,<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> the conclusion of bilateral customs and currency agreements with the <a href="/wiki/Habsburg_monarchy" title="Habsburg monarchy">Habsburg monarchy</a> in 1852, and finally the Customs Treaty with Switzerland in 1923, which was followed by a range of other important bilateral treaties.
</p><p>Post-war economic reconstruction was followed by accession to the Statute of the <a href="/wiki/International_Court_of_Justice" title="International Court of Justice">International Court of Justice</a> in 1950, Liechtenstein signed the CSCE Helsinki Final Act (today's OSCE) together with 34 other states in 1975, Liechtenstein joined the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Europe" title="Council of Europe">Council of Europe</a> in 1978,<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> and Liechtenstein was admitted to the United Nations (UN) on September 18, 1990.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> In 1991, Liechtenstein joined the European Free Trade Association (EFTA) as a full member, and since 1995 Liechtenstein has been a member of the <a href="/wiki/European_Economic_Area" title="European Economic Area">European Economic Area</a> (EEA) and the <a href="/wiki/World_Trade_Organization" title="World Trade Organization">World Trade Organization</a> (WTO).<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> In 2008, Liechtenstein joined the Schengen/Dublin Agreement together with Switzerland. From an economic and integration policy perspective, relations within the framework of the EEA and the EU occupy a special position in Liechtenstein's foreign policy. The Hereditary Prince of Liechtenstein also participates in the annual meetings of the heads of state of the <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_and_territories_where_German_is_an_official_language" title="List of countries and territories where German is an official language">German-speaking countries</a> (consisting of EU and non-EU members).<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Relations with Switzerland are particularly extensive because of the close cooperation in many areas; Switzerland performs tasks in some places that would be difficult for the <a href="/wiki/Principality" title="Principality">Principality</a> to handle on its own because of its small size. Since 2000, Switzerland has appointed an ambassador to Liechtenstein, but he resides in Bern. Liechtenstein's consular representation has been mostly handled by Switzerland since the <a href="/wiki/Customs" title="Customs">Customs</a> Treaty with Switzerland of 1923.
</p><p>Liechtenstein maintains direct diplomatic missions in <a href="/wiki/Vienna" title="Vienna">Vienna</a>, Bern, Berlin, Brussels, Strasbourg, and <a href="/wiki/Washington,_D.C." title="Washington, D.C.">Washington, D.C.</a>, as well as Permanent Missions in New York and Geneva to the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations" title="United Nations">United Nations</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> Currently, diplomatic missions from 78 countries are accredited to Liechtenstein, but mostly reside in Bern. The <a href="/wiki/Diplomatic_mission" title="Diplomatic mission">Embassy</a> in Brussels coordinates contacts with the European Union, Belgium, and also the <a href="/wiki/Holy_See" title="Holy See">Holy See</a>.
</p><p>For a long time, diplomatic relations with Germany were maintained through a non-resident ambassador; that is, a contact person who was not permanently resident in Germany. Since 2002, however, Liechtenstein has had a permanent <a href="/wiki/Ambassador" title="Ambassador">ambassador</a> in Berlin, while the German embassy in Switzerland is also responsible for the Principality. Liechtenstein's Ministry of Foreign Affairs considers the contacts to be extremely fruitful and important for the country's development, especially on the economic level.
</p><p>Conflicts over the handling of <a href="/wiki/Bank" title="Bank">banking</a> and tax data have repeatedly strained relations with Germany. On 2 September 2009, Liechtenstein and Germany signed an agreement on cooperation and the exchange of information in tax matters. The text of the agreement followed the OECD model agreement and provides for an exchange of information on tax matters upon request as of the 2010 tax year. In addition, Liechtenstein regards Germany as an important partner in safeguarding its interests in European integration. At the cultural level, project sponsorship plays a particularly important role. For example, the Hilti Foundation financed the exhibition "Egypt's Sunken Treasures" in Berlin, and the state donated 20,000 euros following the fire at the Duchess Anna Amalia Library in <a href="/wiki/Weimar" title="Weimar">Weimar</a>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Geography">Geography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Geography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Geography_of_Liechtenstein" title="Geography of Liechtenstein">Geography of Liechtenstein</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Rhein_bei_Balzers_-_Blick_auf_Gonzen.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Rhein_bei_Balzers_-_Blick_auf_Gonzen.JPG/220px-Rhein_bei_Balzers_-_Blick_auf_Gonzen.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Rhein_bei_Balzers_-_Blick_auf_Gonzen.JPG/330px-Rhein_bei_Balzers_-_Blick_auf_Gonzen.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Rhein_bei_Balzers_-_Blick_auf_Gonzen.JPG/440px-Rhein_bei_Balzers_-_Blick_auf_Gonzen.JPG 2x" data-file-width="4000" data-file-height="3000" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Rhine" title="Rhine">Rhine</a>: border between Liechtenstein and Switzerland (view towards the <a href="/wiki/Swiss_Alps" title="Swiss Alps">Swiss Alps</a>)</figcaption></figure>
<p>Liechtenstein is situated in the Upper <a href="/wiki/Rhine" title="Rhine">Rhine</a> valley of the European <a href="/wiki/Alps" title="Alps">Alps</a> and is bordered to the east by the Austrian state of <a href="/wiki/Vorarlberg" title="Vorarlberg">Vorarlberg</a>, to the south by the <a href="/wiki/Canton_of_Grisons" class="mw-redirect" title="Canton of Grisons">canton of Grisons</a> (Switzerland) and to the west by the <a href="/wiki/Canton_of_St._Gallen" title="Canton of St. Gallen">canton of St. Gallen</a> (Switzerland). The Rhine forms the entire western border of Liechtenstein. Measured south to north the country is about 24&#160;km (15&#160;mi) long. Its highest point, the <a href="/wiki/Grauspitz" title="Grauspitz">Grauspitz</a>, is 2,599&#160;m (8,527&#160;ft). Despite its Alpine location, prevailing southerly winds make the climate comparatively mild. In winter, the mountain slopes are well suited to winter sports.
</p><p>New <a href="/wiki/Surveying" title="Surveying">surveys</a> using more accurate measurements of the country's borders in 2006 have set its area at 160&#160;km<sup>2</sup> (62&#160;sq&#160;mi), with borders of 77.9&#160;km (48.4&#160;mi).<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> Liechtenstein's borders are 1.9&#160;km (1.2&#160;mi) longer than previously thought.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Liechtenstein is one of the world's two <a href="/wiki/Doubly_landlocked_country" class="mw-redirect" title="Doubly landlocked country">doubly landlocked countries</a><sup id="cite_ref-LiechCIA_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LiechCIA-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> – countries wholly surrounded by other landlocked countries (the other is <a href="/wiki/Uzbekistan" title="Uzbekistan">Uzbekistan</a>). Liechtenstein is the <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_area" title="List of countries and dependencies by area">sixth-smallest</a> independent nation in the world by area.
</p><p>The principality of Liechtenstein is <a href="/wiki/Municipalities_of_Liechtenstein" title="Municipalities of Liechtenstein">divided into 11 communes</a> called <i>Gemeinden</i> (singular <i>Gemeinde</i>). The <i>Gemeinden</i> mostly consist of only a single town or village. Five of them (<a href="/wiki/Eschen" title="Eschen">Eschen</a>, <a href="/wiki/Gamprin" title="Gamprin">Gamprin</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mauren" title="Mauren">Mauren</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ruggell" title="Ruggell">Ruggell</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Schellenberg" title="Schellenberg">Schellenberg</a>) fall within the electoral district <i>Unterland</i> (the lower county), and the remainder (<a href="/wiki/Balzers" title="Balzers">Balzers</a>, <a href="/wiki/Planken" title="Planken">Planken</a>, <a href="/wiki/Schaan" title="Schaan">Schaan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Triesen" title="Triesen">Triesen</a>, <a href="/wiki/Triesenberg" title="Triesenberg">Triesenberg</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Vaduz" title="Vaduz">Vaduz</a>) within <i>Oberland</i> (the upper county).
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Climate">Climate</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Climate">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Despite its <a href="/wiki/Alpine_climate" title="Alpine climate">alpine</a> location, the prevailing southerly winds temper Liechtenstein's climate. Its climate is continental, with cloudy and cold winters, with frequent rain and snowfall. Summers are cool to slightly warm, cloudy, and humid.
</p><p>The country's climate is relatively mild despite its mountainous location. It is strongly influenced by the action of <a href="/wiki/Foehn" class="mw-redirect" title="Foehn">foehn</a> (warm and dry autumn wind), so the vegetation period is prolonged in spring and autumn and temperatures around 15&#160;°C (59&#160;°F) due to the strong foehn are not uncommon even in winter. The mountain ranges of <a href="/wiki/Switzerland" title="Switzerland">Switzerland</a> and the Vorarlberg upstream protect from the cold polar and Atlantic air, creating a typical alpine inland protective layer. The principality has orchards with leafy meadows and a long tradition of <a href="/wiki/Viticulture" title="Viticulture">viticulture</a>. Liechtenstein's small land area hardly plays a role in climatic differences, but the vertical division into different altitudes is of great importance, so that significant climatic differences arise.
</p><p>In <a href="/wiki/Winter" title="Winter">winter</a> the temperature rarely drops below −15&#160;°C (5&#160;°F), while in summer the average temperatures range between 20 and 28&#160;°C (68 and 82&#160;°F). Annual precipitation measurements amount to an average of about 900 to 1,200 millimetres (35 to 47&#160;in), in the direct alpine region, however, <a href="/wiki/Precipitation" title="Precipitation">precipitation</a> is often up to 1,900&#160;mm (75&#160;in). The average duration of sunshine is about 1600 hours per year.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Vaduz,_Liechtenstein._Schweize_Gebirge_im_Hintergrund_-_panoramio.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Vaduz%2C_Liechtenstein._Schweize_Gebirge_im_Hintergrund_-_panoramio.jpg/250px-Vaduz%2C_Liechtenstein._Schweize_Gebirge_im_Hintergrund_-_panoramio.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="141" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Vaduz%2C_Liechtenstein._Schweize_Gebirge_im_Hintergrund_-_panoramio.jpg/375px-Vaduz%2C_Liechtenstein._Schweize_Gebirge_im_Hintergrund_-_panoramio.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Vaduz%2C_Liechtenstein._Schweize_Gebirge_im_Hintergrund_-_panoramio.jpg/500px-Vaduz%2C_Liechtenstein._Schweize_Gebirge_im_Hintergrund_-_panoramio.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1920" data-file-height="1080" /></a><figcaption>Panorama of <a href="/wiki/Vaduz" title="Vaduz">Vaduz</a>, capital of Liechtenstein</figcaption></figure>
