<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Rivers_and_lakes">Rivers and lakes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Rivers and lakes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Rhine" title="Rhine">Rhine</a> is the longest and largest <a href="/wiki/Body_of_water" title="Body of water">body of water</a> in Liechtenstein. With a length of approximately 27 kilometres (17&#160;mi), it represents the <a href="/wiki/Natural_border" title="Natural border">natural border</a> with <a href="/wiki/Switzerland" title="Switzerland">Switzerland</a> and is of great importance for Liechtenstein's water supply. Furthermore, the Rhine is an important recreational area for the population. At 10 kilometres (6&#160;mi), the Samina is the second longest river in the <a href="/wiki/Principality" title="Principality">Principality</a>. The troubled river begins at Triesenberg and flows into the Ill in Austria (near Feldkirch).
</p><p>The only naturally formed lake in Liechtenstein is the Gampriner Seelein, which was not formed until 1927 by a <a href="/wiki/Flooding" class="mw-redirect" title="Flooding">flooding</a> of the Rhine with enormous erosion. In addition, there are other artificially created <a href="/wiki/Lakes" class="mw-redirect" title="Lakes">lakes</a>, which are mainly used to generate <a href="/wiki/Electricity" title="Electricity">electricity</a>. One of them is the Steg Reservoir, the largest lake in Liechtenstein.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Mountains">Mountains</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Mountains">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>About half of Liechtenstein's territory is mountainous.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> Liechtenstein lies entirely in the <a href="/wiki/R%C3%A4tikon" title="Rätikon">Rhaetikon</a> and is thus – depending on the classification of the Alps – assigned either to the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Alps" title="Eastern Alps">Eastern Alps</a> (two-part division of the Alps) or to the Central Alps (three-part division of the Alps).
</p><p>The highest point of Liechtenstein is the Vordere Grauspitz (Vordergrauspitz) with an altitude of 2,599&#160;m (8,527&#160;ft) above sea level, while the lowest point is the <a href="/wiki/Ruggeller_Riet" title="Ruggeller Riet">Ruggeller Riet</a> with an altitude of 430 metres (1,410&#160;ft) above sea level.
</p><p>In total, there are 32 mountains in Liechtenstein with an altitude of at least 2,000 metres (6,600&#160;ft). The Falknishorn, at 2,452 metres (8,045&#160;ft) above sea level, is the fifth highest mountain in Liechtenstein and represents the southernmost point of the country. The Liechtenstein-Graubünden-Vorarlberg border triangle is the <a href="/wiki/Naafkopf" title="Naafkopf">Naafkopf</a> (2,570&#160;m (8,430&#160;ft) above sea level).<sup id="cite_ref-:0_58-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In addition to the peaks of the Alpine chain,<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> which belong to the Limestone Alps, two <a href="/wiki/Inselberg" title="Inselberg">inselbergs</a>, Fläscherberg (1,135&#160;m or 3,724&#160;ft <a href="/wiki/Above_sea_level" class="mw-redirect" title="Above sea level">above sea level</a>) in the south and <a href="/wiki/Eschnerberg" title="Eschnerberg">Eschnerberg</a> (698&#160;m or 2,290&#160;ft) in the north, rise from the Rhine Valley and belong to the Helvetic cover or <a href="/wiki/Flysch" title="Flysch">flysch</a> zone of the Alps.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> Eschnerberg represents an important settlement area in the Liechtenstein Unterland.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Economy">Economy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Economy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Economy_of_Liechtenstein" title="Economy of Liechtenstein">Economy of Liechtenstein</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Vaduz_Zentrum.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/Vaduz_Zentrum.jpg/220px-Vaduz_Zentrum.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="163" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/Vaduz_Zentrum.jpg/330px-Vaduz_Zentrum.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/Vaduz_Zentrum.jpg/440px-Vaduz_Zentrum.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3735" data-file-height="2773" /></a><figcaption>Looking southward at Vaduz city centre</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:5_kronen_Johann_II_of_Liechtenstein_1904.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/5_kronen_Johann_II_of_Liechtenstein_1904.png/250px-5_kronen_Johann_II_of_Liechtenstein_1904.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="124" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/5_kronen_Johann_II_of_Liechtenstein_1904.png/375px-5_kronen_Johann_II_of_Liechtenstein_1904.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/5_kronen_Johann_II_of_Liechtenstein_1904.png/500px-5_kronen_Johann_II_of_Liechtenstein_1904.png 2x" data-file-width="2984" data-file-height="1486" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Silver_coin" title="Silver coin">Silver coin</a>: 5 kronen of Liechtenstein, 1904, the front of the coin is a portrait of Johann II</figcaption></figure>
<p>Despite its limited natural resources, Liechtenstein is one of the few countries in the world with more registered companies than citizens; it has developed a prosperous, highly industrialized free-enterprise economy and boasts a financial service sector as well as a living standard that compares favourably with those of the urban areas of Liechtenstein's much larger European neighbours.
</p><p>Liechtenstein participates in a <a href="/wiki/Customs_union" title="Customs union">customs union</a> with Switzerland and employs the <a href="/wiki/Swiss_franc" title="Swiss franc">Swiss franc</a> as the national currency. The country imports about 85% of its energy. Liechtenstein has been a member of the <a href="/wiki/European_Economic_Area" title="European Economic Area">European Economic Area</a> (an organization serving as a bridge between the <a href="/wiki/European_Free_Trade_Association" title="European Free Trade Association">European Free Trade Association</a> (EFTA) and the <a href="/wiki/European_Union" title="European Union">European Union</a>) since May 1995.
</p><p>The government is working to harmonize its economic policies with those of an integrated Europe. In 2008, the unemployment rate was 1.5%. Liechtenstein has only one hospital, the Liechtensteinisches Landesspital in Vaduz. As of 2014 the <a href="/wiki/CIA_World_Factbook" class="mw-redirect" title="CIA World Factbook">CIA World Factbook</a> estimated the gross domestic product (GDP) on a <a href="/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity" title="Purchasing power parity">purchasing power parity</a> basis to be $4.978&#160;billion. As of 2009 the estimate per capita was $139,100, the highest listed for the world.<sup id="cite_ref-LiechCIA_55-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LiechCIA-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Industries include electronics, textiles, precision instruments, metal manufacturing, power tools, anchor bolts, calculators, pharmaceuticals and food products. Its most recognizable international company and largest employer is <a href="/wiki/Hilti" title="Hilti">Hilti</a>, a manufacturer of <a href="/wiki/Nail_gun" title="Nail gun">direct fastening systems</a> and other high-end power tools. Many cultivated fields and small farms are found both in the Oberland and Unterland. Liechtenstein produces wheat, barley, corn, potatoes, dairy products, livestock and <a href="/wiki/Liechtenstein_wine" title="Liechtenstein wine">wine</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Taxation">Taxation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Taxation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Grenze_Liechtenstein,_Balzers.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5f/Grenze_Liechtenstein%2C_Balzers.jpg/220px-Grenze_Liechtenstein%2C_Balzers.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="162" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5f/Grenze_Liechtenstein%2C_Balzers.jpg/330px-Grenze_Liechtenstein%2C_Balzers.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5f/Grenze_Liechtenstein%2C_Balzers.jpg/440px-Grenze_Liechtenstein%2C_Balzers.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3257" data-file-height="2395" /></a><figcaption>Since 1923, there has been no <a href="/wiki/Border_control" title="Border control">border control</a> between Liechtenstein and <a href="/wiki/Switzerland" title="Switzerland">Switzerland</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The government of Liechtenstein taxes personal income, business income and principal (wealth). The basic rate of <a href="/wiki/Income_tax#Personal" title="Income tax">personal income tax</a> is 1.2%. When combined with the additional income tax imposed by the communes, the combined income tax rate is 17.82%.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> An additional income tax of 4.3% is levied on all employees under the country's <a href="/wiki/Social_security" class="mw-redirect" title="Social security">social security</a> programme. This rate is higher for the self-employed, up to a maximum of 11%, making the maximum income tax rate about 29% in total. The basic <a href="/wiki/Wealth_tax" title="Wealth tax">tax rate on wealth</a> is 0.06% per annum, and the combined total rate is 0.89%. The tax rate on corporate profits is 12.5%.<sup id="cite_ref-LiechCIA_55-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LiechCIA-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Liechtenstein's <a href="/wiki/Gift_tax" title="Gift tax">gift</a> and <a href="/wiki/Inheritance_tax" title="Inheritance tax">estate taxes</a> vary depending on the relationship the recipient has to the giver and the amount of the inheritance. The tax ranges between 0.5% and 0.75% for spouses and children and 18% to 27% for non-related recipients. The estate tax is progressive.
</p><p>Liechtenstein has previously received significant revenues from <i>Stiftungen</i> ("foundations"), financial entities created to hide the true owner of nonresident foreigners' financial holdings. The foundation is registered in the name of a Liechtensteiner, often a lawyer. This set of laws used to make Liechtenstein a popular <a href="/wiki/Tax_haven" title="Tax haven">tax haven</a> for extremely wealthy individuals and businesses attempting to avoid or evade taxes in their home countries.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> In recent years, Liechtenstein has displayed stronger determination to prosecute international money launderers and worked to promote an image as a legitimate finance centre. In February 2008, the country's <a href="/wiki/LGT_Group" title="LGT Group">LGT Bank</a> was implicated in a <a href="/wiki/2008_Liechtenstein_tax_affair" title="2008 Liechtenstein tax affair">tax-fraud scandal in Germany</a>, which strained the ruling family's relationship with the German government. Crown Prince Alois has accused the German government of trafficking in stolen goods, referring to its $7.3&#160;million purchase of private banking information offered by a former employee of LGT Group.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/United_States_Senate" title="United States Senate">United States Senate</a>'s subcommittee on tax haven banks said that the LGT bank, owned by the princely family, and on whose board they serve, "is a willing partner, and an aider and abettor to clients trying to evade taxes, dodge creditors or defy court orders".<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Hilti_Schaan.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/Hilti_Schaan.jpg/220px-Hilti_Schaan.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/Hilti_Schaan.jpg/330px-Hilti_Schaan.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/Hilti_Schaan.jpg/440px-Hilti_Schaan.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2244" data-file-height="1488" /></a><figcaption>Headquarters of <a href="/wiki/Hilti" title="Hilti">Hilti</a> Corporation in <a href="/wiki/Schaan" title="Schaan">Schaan</a>, Liechtenstein</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/2008_Liechtenstein_tax_affair" title="2008 Liechtenstein tax affair">2008 Liechtenstein tax affair</a> is a series of tax investigations in numerous countries whose governments suspect that some of their citizens have evaded tax obligations by using banks and trusts in Liechtenstein; the affair broke open with the biggest complex of investigations ever initiated for tax evasion in Germany.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> It was also seen as an attempt to put pressure on Liechtenstein, then one of the remaining <a href="/wiki/FATF_blacklist" class="mw-redirect" title="FATF blacklist">uncooperative tax havens</a>—along with <a href="/wiki/Andorra" title="Andorra">Andorra</a> and <a href="/wiki/Monaco" title="Monaco">Monaco</a>—as identified by the Paris-based <a href="/wiki/Organisation_for_Economic_Co-operation_and_Development" class="mw-redirect" title="Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development">Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development</a> in 2007.<sup id="cite_ref-wsj0219_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-wsj0219-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> On 27 May 2009 the OECD removed Liechtenstein from the blacklist of uncooperative countries.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In August 2009, the British government department <a href="/wiki/HM_Revenue_%26_Customs" class="mw-redirect" title="HM Revenue &amp; Customs">HM Revenue &amp; Customs</a> agreed with Liechtenstein to start exchanging information. It is believed that up to 5,000 British investors have roughly £3&#160;billion deposited in accounts and trusts in the country.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In October 2015, the European Union and Liechtenstein signed a tax agreement to ensure the automatic exchange of financial information in case of tax disputes. The collection of data started in 2016. It is another step to bring the principality in line with other European countries regarding its taxation of private individuals and corporate assets.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Tourism">Tourism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Tourism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Tourism accounts for a large portion of Liechtenstein's economy.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Demographics">Demographics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Demographics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Demographics_of_Liechtenstein" title="Demographics of Liechtenstein">Demographics of Liechtenstein</a></div>
<p>With a population of 39,315 as of 31 December 2021,<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> Liechtenstein is <a href="/wiki/List_of_European_countries_by_population" title="List of European countries by population">Europe's fourth-smallest country</a>; <a href="/wiki/Vatican_City" title="Vatican City">Vatican City</a>, <a href="/wiki/San_Marino" title="San Marino">San Marino</a> and <a href="/wiki/Monaco" title="Monaco">Monaco</a> have fewer residents. Its population is primarily <a href="/wiki/Alemannic_German" title="Alemannic German">Alemannic</a>-speaking, although one third is foreign-born, primarily German speakers from <a href="/wiki/Germany" title="Germany">Germany</a>, <a href="/wiki/Austria" title="Austria">Austria</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Switzerland" title="Switzerland">Switzerland</a>, along with other Swiss, Italians, and <a href="/wiki/Turkish_people" title="Turkish people">Turks</a>. Foreign-born people make up two-thirds of the country's workforce.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Liechtensteiners have an average life expectancy at birth of 82.0 years, subdividing as male: 79.8 years, female: 84.8 years (2018 est.). The infant mortality rate is 4.2 per 1,000 live births, according to 2018 estimates.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Languages">Languages</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Languages">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The official language is German, spoken by 92% of the population as their main language in 2020. 73% of Liechtenstein's population speak an <a href="/wiki/Alemannic_German" title="Alemannic German">Alemannic</a> <a href="/wiki/Dialect" title="Dialect">dialect</a> of German at home that is highly divergent from <a href="/wiki/Standard_German" title="Standard German">Standard German</a> but closely related to dialects spoken in neighbouring regions such as Switzerland and <a href="/wiki/Vorarlberg" title="Vorarlberg">Vorarlberg</a>, Austria.<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> In <a href="/wiki/Triesenberg" title="Triesenberg">Triesenberg</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Walser_German" title="Walser German">Walser German</a> dialect promoted by the municipality is spoken. <a href="/wiki/Swiss_Standard_German" title="Swiss Standard German">Swiss Standard German</a> is also understood and spoken by most Liechtensteiners.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Religion">Religion</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Religion">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Religion_in_Liechtenstein" title="Religion in Liechtenstein">Religion in Liechtenstein</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Church_vaduz.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ab/Church_vaduz.png/170px-Church_vaduz.png" decoding="async" width="170" height="227" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ab/Church_vaduz.png/255px-Church_vaduz.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ab/Church_vaduz.png/340px-Church_vaduz.png 2x" data-file-width="1944" data-file-height="2592" /></a><figcaption>St. Florin Catholic Cathedral in Vaduz</figcaption></figure>
<p>According to the <a href="/wiki/Constitution_of_Liechtenstein" title="Constitution of Liechtenstein">Constitution of Liechtenstein</a>, <a href="/wiki/Catholicism" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholicism">Catholicism</a> is its official <a href="/wiki/State_religion" title="State religion">state religion</a>:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>The Catholic Church is the State Church and as such shall enjoy the full protection of the State</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8202;<cite><a href="/wiki/Constitution_of_Liechtenstein" title="Constitution of Liechtenstein">Constitution of Liechtenstein</a><sup id="cite_ref-Temperman2010_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Temperman2010-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup></cite></div></blockquote>
<p>Liechtenstein offers protection to adherents of all religions, and considers the "religious interests of the people" a priority of the government.<sup id="cite_ref-Temperman2010_74-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Temperman2010-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> In Liechtenstein's schools, although exceptions are allowed, religious education in Catholicism or <a href="/wiki/Protestantism" title="Protestantism">Protestantism</a> (either <a href="/wiki/Lutheran" class="mw-redirect" title="Lutheran">Lutheran</a> or <a href="/wiki/Calvinist" class="mw-redirect" title="Calvinist">Calvinist</a>, or both) is legally required.<sup id="cite_ref-Piano2009_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Piano2009-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Tax_exemption" title="Tax exemption">Tax exemption</a> is granted by the government to religious organizations.<sup id="cite_ref-Piano2009_75-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Piano2009-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> According to the <a href="/wiki/Pew_Research_Center" title="Pew Research Center">Pew Research Center</a>, social conflict caused by religious hostilities is low in Liechtenstein, and so is government restriction on the practice of religion.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to the 2010 census, 85.8% of the total population were Christian, of whom 75.9% adhered to the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic</a> faith, constituted in the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Vaduz" title="Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Vaduz">Catholic Archdiocese of Vaduz</a>, while 9.6% were either <a href="/wiki/Protestant" class="mw-redirect" title="Protestant">Protestant</a>, mainly organized in the <a href="/wiki/Evangelical_Church_in_Liechtenstein" title="Evangelical Church in Liechtenstein">Evangelical Church in Liechtenstein</a> (a <a href="/wiki/United_church" class="mw-redirect" title="United church">United church</a>, Lutheran &amp; Reformed) and the <a href="/wiki/Evangelical_Lutheran_Church_in_Liechtenstein" class="mw-redirect" title="Evangelical Lutheran Church in Liechtenstein">Evangelical Lutheran Church in Liechtenstein</a>, or <a href="/wiki/Orthodoxy" title="Orthodoxy">Orthodox</a>, mainly organized in the Christian-Orthodox Church.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> The largest minority religion is <a href="/wiki/Islam" title="Islam">Islam</a> (5.4% of the total population).<sup id="cite_ref-r2_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-r2-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2020, 72% of the population were Catholic, 10% was Protestant and 6% was Muslim; 5% had no religious beliefs.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> 
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Education">Education</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Education">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Education_in_Liechtenstein" title="Education in Liechtenstein">Education in Liechtenstein</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:University_of_Liechtenstein.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/University_of_Liechtenstein.JPG/220px-University_of_Liechtenstein.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/University_of_Liechtenstein.JPG/330px-University_of_Liechtenstein.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/University_of_Liechtenstein.JPG/440px-University_of_Liechtenstein.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1984" data-file-height="1488" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/University_of_Liechtenstein" title="University of Liechtenstein">University of Liechtenstein</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>The literacy rate of Liechtenstein is 100%.<sup id="cite_ref-LiechCIA_55-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LiechCIA-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> In 2006 <a href="/wiki/Programme_for_International_Student_Assessment" title="Programme for International Student Assessment">Programme for International Student Assessment</a> report, coordinated by the <a href="/wiki/Organisation_for_Economic_Co-operation_and_Development" class="mw-redirect" title="Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development">Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development</a>, ranked Liechtenstein's education as the 10th-best in the world.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> In 2012, Liechtenstein had the highest PISA scores of any European country.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Within Liechtenstein, there are four main centres for higher education:
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/University_of_Liechtenstein" title="University of Liechtenstein">University of Liechtenstein</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Private_University_in_the_Principality_of_Liechtenstein" title="Private University in the Principality of Liechtenstein">Private University in the Principality of Liechtenstein</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Liechtenstein_Institute" title="Liechtenstein Institute">Liechtenstein Institute</a></li>
<li><a href="/w/index.php?title=International_Academy_of_Philosophy,_Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="International Academy of Philosophy, Liechtenstein (page does not exist)">International Academy of Philosophy, Liechtenstein</a></li></ul>
<p>There are nine public high schools in the country. These include:
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Liechtensteinisches_Gymnasium" title="Liechtensteinisches Gymnasium">Liechtensteinisches Gymnasium</a> in Vaduz.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Realschule" title="Realschule">Realschule</a> Vaduz and <a href="/wiki/Oberschule" class="mw-redirect" title="Oberschule">Oberschule</a> Vaduz, in the <a href="/w/index.php?title=Schulzentrum_M%C3%BChleholz_II&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Schulzentrum Mühleholz II (page does not exist)">Schulzentrum Mühleholz II</a> in Vaduz<sup id="cite_ref-WeiterSchaan_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WeiterSchaan-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Realschule Schaan and Sportschule Liechtenstein in <a href="/wiki/Schaan" title="Schaan">Schaan</a><sup id="cite_ref-WeiterSchaan_82-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WeiterSchaan-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Transport">Transport</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Transport">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Hubschrauberlandeplatz_Balzers_Liechtenstein.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/Hubschrauberlandeplatz_Balzers_Liechtenstein.jpg/220px-Hubschrauberlandeplatz_Balzers_Liechtenstein.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="144" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/Hubschrauberlandeplatz_Balzers_Liechtenstein.jpg/330px-Hubschrauberlandeplatz_Balzers_Liechtenstein.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/Hubschrauberlandeplatz_Balzers_Liechtenstein.jpg/440px-Hubschrauberlandeplatz_Balzers_Liechtenstein.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3962" data-file-height="2597" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Balzers_Heliport" class="mw-redirect" title="Balzers Heliport">Balzers Heliport</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>There are about 250 kilometres (155&#160;mi) of paved roadway within Liechtenstein, with 90&#160;km (56&#160;mi) of marked bicycle paths.
</p><p>A 9.5&#160;km (6&#160;mi) railway connects Austria and Switzerland through Liechtenstein. The <a href="/wiki/Rail_transport_in_Liechtenstein" title="Rail transport in Liechtenstein">country's railways</a> are administered by the <a href="/wiki/Austrian_Federal_Railways" class="mw-redirect" title="Austrian Federal Railways">Austrian Federal Railways</a> as part of the route between <a href="/wiki/Feldkirch,_Vorarlberg" title="Feldkirch, Vorarlberg">Feldkirch</a>, <a href="/wiki/Austria" title="Austria">Austria</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Buchs,_St._Gallen" title="Buchs, St. Gallen">Buchs</a>, Switzerland. Liechtenstein is nominally within the Austrian Verkehrsverbund Vorarlberg<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> tariff region.
</p><p>There are four railway stations in Liechtenstein, namely <a href="/wiki/Schaan-Vaduz_railway_station" title="Schaan-Vaduz railway station">Schaan-Vaduz</a>, <a href="/wiki/Forst_Hilti_railway_station" title="Forst Hilti railway station">Forst Hilti</a>, <a href="/wiki/Nendeln_railway_station" title="Nendeln railway station">Nendeln</a> and <a href="/wiki/Schaanwald_railway_station" title="Schaanwald railway station">Schaanwald</a>, served by an irregularly stopping train service between Feldkirch and Buchs provided by <a href="/wiki/Austrian_Federal_Railways" class="mw-redirect" title="Austrian Federal Railways">Austrian Federal Railways</a>. While <a href="/wiki/EuroCity" title="EuroCity">EuroCity</a> and other long-distance international trains also travel along the route, they do not normally stop at the stations within the borders of Liechtenstein.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Liechtenstein_Bus" title="Liechtenstein Bus">Liechtenstein Bus</a> is a subsidiary of the <a href="/wiki/PostBus_Switzerland" title="PostBus Switzerland">Swiss Postbus system</a>, but separately run, and connects to the Swiss bus network at <a href="/wiki/Buchs,_St._Gallen" title="Buchs, St. Gallen">Buchs</a> and at <a href="/wiki/Sargans" title="Sargans">Sargans</a>. Buses also run to the Austrian town of Feldkirch.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Liechtenstein is one of only a few <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_without_an_airport" title="List of countries without an airport">countries without an airport</a>. The nearest large airport is <a href="/wiki/Zurich_Airport" title="Zurich Airport">Zurich Airport</a> near <a href="/wiki/Z%C3%BCrich" title="Zürich">Zürich</a>, <a href="/wiki/Switzerland" title="Switzerland">Switzerland</a> (130&#160;km or 80 miles by road). The nearest small airport is <a href="/wiki/St._Gallen%E2%80%93Altenrhein_Airport" title="St. Gallen–Altenrhein Airport">St. Gallen Airport</a> (50&#160;km or 30 miles). <a href="/wiki/Friedrichshafen_Airport" title="Friedrichshafen Airport">Friedrichshafen Airport</a> also provides access to Liechtenstein, as it is 85&#160;km (53 miles) away. <a href="/wiki/Balzers_Heliport" class="mw-redirect" title="Balzers Heliport">Balzers Heliport</a> is<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> available for chartered helicopter flights.
</p>
<div style="clear:both;"></div>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Culture">Culture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Culture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>

</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Culture_of_Liechtenstein" title="Template:Culture of Liechtenstein"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Culture_of_Liechtenstein" title="Template talk:Culture of Liechtenstein"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Culture_of_Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:KML_HAF_aussen_vom_Staedtle_Barbara_Buehler.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/16/KML_HAF_aussen_vom_Staedtle_Barbara_Buehler.jpg/220px-KML_HAF_aussen_vom_Staedtle_Barbara_Buehler.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="180" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/16/KML_HAF_aussen_vom_Staedtle_Barbara_Buehler.jpg/330px-KML_HAF_aussen_vom_Staedtle_Barbara_Buehler.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/16/KML_HAF_aussen_vom_Staedtle_Barbara_Buehler.jpg/440px-KML_HAF_aussen_vom_Staedtle_Barbara_Buehler.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1800" data-file-height="1469" /></a><figcaption>Kunstmuseum (<a href="/wiki/Kunstmuseum_Liechtenstein" title="Kunstmuseum Liechtenstein">Liechtenstein Art Museum</a>)</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Liechtensteinisches_Landesmuseum,_2014.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/13/Liechtensteinisches_Landesmuseum%2C_2014.JPG/220px-Liechtensteinisches_Landesmuseum%2C_2014.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/13/Liechtensteinisches_Landesmuseum%2C_2014.JPG/330px-Liechtensteinisches_Landesmuseum%2C_2014.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/13/Liechtensteinisches_Landesmuseum%2C_2014.JPG/440px-Liechtensteinisches_Landesmuseum%2C_2014.JPG 2x" data-file-width="3225" data-file-height="2151" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Liechtenstein_National_Museum" title="Liechtenstein National Museum">Liechtenstein National Museum</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>As a result of its small size, Liechtenstein has been strongly affected by external cultural influences, most notably those originating in the southern regions of German-speaking Europe, including Austria, Baden-Württemberg, Bavaria, Switzerland, and specifically <a href="/wiki/German_Tyrol" title="German Tyrol">Tirol</a> and Vorarlberg. The Historical Society of the Principality of Liechtenstein plays a role in preserving the culture and history of the country.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>The largest museum is the <a href="/wiki/Kunstmuseum_Liechtenstein" title="Kunstmuseum Liechtenstein">Kunstmuseum Liechtenstein</a>, an international museum of modern and contemporary art with an important international art collection. The building by the Swiss architects Morger, Degelo, and Kerez is a landmark in Vaduz. It was completed in November 2000 and forms a "black box" of tinted concrete and black basalt stone. The museum collection is also the national art collection of Liechtenstein.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>The other important museum is the <a href="/wiki/Liechtenstein_National_Museum" title="Liechtenstein National Museum">Liechtenstein National Museum</a> (<i>Liechtensteinisches Landesmuseum</i>) showing permanent exhibitions on the cultural and natural history of Liechtenstein as well as special exhibitions. There is also a stamp museum, ski museum, and a 500-year-old Rural Lifestyle Museum.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Liechtenstein_State_Library" title="Liechtenstein State Library">Liechtenstein State Library</a> is the library that has <a href="/wiki/Legal_deposit" title="Legal deposit">legal deposit</a> for all books published in the country.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> Poet <a href="/wiki/Ida_Ospelt-Amann" title="Ida Ospelt-Amann">Ida Ospelt-Amann</a> published her works exclusively in the Alemannic dialect of Vaduz.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The most famous historical sites are <a href="/wiki/Vaduz_Castle" title="Vaduz Castle">Vaduz Castle</a>, <a href="/wiki/Gutenberg_Castle" title="Gutenberg Castle">Gutenberg Castle</a>, the Red House, and the ruins of Schellenberg.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>The Private Art Collection of the Prince of Liechtenstein, one of the world's leading private art collections, is shown at the <a href="/wiki/Liechtenstein_Museum" title="Liechtenstein Museum">Liechtenstein Museum</a> in Vienna.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>On the country's national holiday, all subjects are invited to the castle of the head of state. A significant portion of the population attends the national celebration at the castle where speeches are made and complimentary beer is served.<sup id="cite_ref-Letzing_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Letzing-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Music and theatre are important parts of the culture. There are numerous music organizations such as the Liechtenstein Musical Company, the annual Guitar Days, and the International <a href="/wiki/Josef_Gabriel_Rheinberger" class="mw-redirect" title="Josef Gabriel Rheinberger">Josef Gabriel Rheinberger</a> Society, which play in two main theatres.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Media">Media</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Media">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The primary <a href="/wiki/Internet_service_provider" title="Internet service provider">internet service provider</a> and <a href="/wiki/Mobile_network_operator" title="Mobile network operator">mobile network operator</a> of Liechtenstein is Telecom Liechtenstein, located in Schaan.
</p><p>There are two conventional television channels in the country. The private channel <a href="/wiki/1FLTV" class="mw-redirect" title="1FLTV">1FLTV</a> was created in 2008 with a goal of joining the <a href="/wiki/European_Broadcasting_Union" title="European Broadcasting Union">European Broadcasting Union</a>, which it has not accomplished yet. The <i>Landeskanal</i> (<span style="font-size:85%;"><a href="https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landeskanal" class="extiw" title="de:Landeskanal">de</a></span>) is operated by the government's Unit for Information and Communication and carries government proceedings, <a href="/wiki/Public_affairs_(broadcasting)" title="Public affairs (broadcasting)">public affairs</a> programming, and cultural events. Both are seen on local cable providers, along with channels from the other German-speaking countries. The only free television is <a href="/wiki/ORF_(broadcaster)" title="ORF (broadcaster)">ORF</a> from Austria, available via terrestrial overspill of its signal from <a href="/wiki/Vorarlberg" title="Vorarlberg">Vorarlberg</a>.
</p><p>Radio Liechtenstein (<span style="font-size:85%;"><a href="https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Liechtenstein" class="extiw" title="de:Radio Liechtenstein">de</a></span>), which was established in 2004 along with the public-service broadcaster <i><a href="https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liechtensteinischer_Rundfunk" class="extiw" title="de:Liechtensteinischer Rundfunk">Liechtensteinischer Rundfunk</a></i> (LRF) that operates it, is the country's only domestic radio station based in Triesen. Radio Liechtenstein and several programs of the Swiss <a href="/wiki/Schweizer_Radio_und_Fernsehen" title="Schweizer Radio und Fernsehen">SRF</a> are broadcast from the Sender Erbi (<span style="font-size:85%;"><a href="https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sender_Erbi" class="extiw" title="de:Sender Erbi">de</a></span>) overlooking Vaduz. Liechtenstein also has two major newspapers: <i><a href="/wiki/Liechtensteiner_Volksblatt" title="Liechtensteiner Volksblatt">Liechtensteiner Volksblatt</a></i> and <i><a href="/wiki/Liechtensteiner_Vaterland" title="Liechtensteiner Vaterland">Liechtensteiner Vaterland</a></i>.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Amateur_radio" title="Amateur radio">Amateur radio</a> is a hobby of some nationals and visitors. However, unlike virtually every other sovereign nation, Liechtenstein does not have its own <a href="/wiki/ITU_prefix" title="ITU prefix">ITU prefix</a>. Conventionally, amateurs are issued call signs with the Swiss prefix "HB", followed by "0" or "L".
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Sports">Sports</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Sports">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Marco_B%C3%BCchel.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/Marco_B%C3%BCchel.jpg/170px-Marco_B%C3%BCchel.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="178" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/Marco_B%C3%BCchel.jpg/255px-Marco_B%C3%BCchel.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/Marco_B%C3%BCchel.jpg/340px-Marco_B%C3%BCchel.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2112" data-file-height="2208" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Marco_B%C3%BCchel" title="Marco Büchel">Marco Büchel</a>, the first Liechtensteiner alpine skier to compete at six <a href="/wiki/Winter_Olympic_Games" title="Winter Olympic Games">Winter Olympics</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Liechtenstein <a href="/wiki/Association_football" title="Association football">football</a> teams play in the Swiss football leagues. The <a href="/wiki/Liechtenstein_Football_Cup" title="Liechtenstein Football Cup">Liechtenstein Football Cup</a> allows access for one Liechtenstein team each year to the <a href="/wiki/UEFA_Europa_Conference_League" title="UEFA Europa Conference League">UEFA Europa Conference League</a>; <a href="/wiki/FC_Vaduz" title="FC Vaduz">FC Vaduz</a>, a team playing in the <a href="/wiki/Swiss_Challenge_League" title="Swiss Challenge League">Swiss Challenge League</a>, the second division in Swiss football, is the most successful team in the Cup, and scored their greatest success in the <a href="/wiki/1996%E2%80%9397_UEFA_Cup_Winners%27_Cup" title="1996–97 UEFA Cup Winners&#39; Cup">European Cup Winners' Cup in 1996</a> when they drew with and defeated the Latvian team <a href="/wiki/FK_Jelgava" title="FK Jelgava">FC Universitate Riga</a> by 1–1 and 4–2, to go on to a lucrative fixture against <a href="/wiki/Paris_Saint-Germain_F.C." title="Paris Saint-Germain F.C.">Paris Saint-Germain F.C.</a>, which they lost 0–3 and 0–4.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Liechtenstein_national_football_team" title="Liechtenstein national football team">Liechtenstein national football team</a> is regarded as an easy target for any team drawn against them; this was the basis for a book about Liechtenstein's unsuccessful qualifying campaign for the <a href="/wiki/2002_FIFA_World_Cup" title="2002 FIFA World Cup">2002 World Cup</a> by British author <a href="/wiki/Charlie_Connelly" title="Charlie Connelly">Charlie Connelly</a>. In one surprising week during autumn 2004, however, the team managed a 2–2 draw with <a href="/wiki/Portugal_national_football_team" title="Portugal national football team">Portugal</a>, who only a few months earlier had been the losing finalists in the <a href="/wiki/UEFA_Euro_2004" title="UEFA Euro 2004">European Championships</a>. Four days later, the Liechtenstein team traveled to Luxembourg, where they defeated <a href="/wiki/Luxembourg_national_football_team" title="Luxembourg national football team">the home team</a> 4–0 in a <a href="/wiki/2006_FIFA_World_Cup" title="2006 FIFA World Cup">2006 World Cup</a> qualifying match. In the qualification stage of the European Championship 2008, Liechtenstein beat Latvia 1–0, which prompted the Latvian coach's resignation. They went on to beat Iceland 3–0 on 17 October 2007, which is considered one of the most dramatic losses of the Icelandic national football team. On 7 September 2010, they came within seconds of a 1–1 draw against <a href="/wiki/Scotland_national_football_team" title="Scotland national football team">Scotland</a> in Glasgow, having led 1–0 earlier in the second half, but Liechtenstein lost 2–1 thanks to a goal by <a href="/wiki/Stephen_McManus" title="Stephen McManus">Stephen McManus</a> in the 97th minute. On 3 June 2011, Liechtenstein defeated <a href="/wiki/Lithuania_national_football_team" title="Lithuania national football team">Lithuania</a> 2–0. On 15 November 2014, Liechtenstein defeated <a href="/wiki/Moldova_national_football_team" title="Moldova national football team">Moldova</a> 0–1 with <a href="/wiki/Franz_Burgmeier" title="Franz Burgmeier">Franz Burgmeier</a>'s late free kick goal in <a href="/wiki/Chi%C8%99in%C4%83u" title="Chișinău">Chișinău</a>.
</p><p>As an <a href="/wiki/Alps" title="Alps">alpine</a> country, the main sporting opportunity for Liechtensteiners to excel is in winter sports such as <a href="/wiki/Downhill_(ski_competition)" title="Downhill (ski competition)">downhill skiing</a>: the country's single ski area is <a href="/wiki/Malbun" title="Malbun">Malbun</a>. <a href="/wiki/Hanni_Wenzel" title="Hanni Wenzel">Hanni Wenzel</a> won two gold medals and one silver medal in the 1980 <a href="/wiki/Winter_Olympic_Games" title="Winter Olympic Games">Winter Olympics</a> (she won bronze in 1976), her brother <a href="/wiki/Andreas_Wenzel" title="Andreas Wenzel">Andreas</a> won one silver medal in 1980 and one bronze medal in 1984 in the <a href="/wiki/Giant_slalom_skiing" class="mw-redirect" title="Giant slalom skiing">giant slalom</a> event, and her daughter <a href="/wiki/Tina_Weirather" title="Tina Weirather">Tina Weirather</a> won a bronze medal in 2018 in the <a href="/wiki/Super-G" title="Super-G">Super-G</a>. With ten medals overall (all in alpine skiing), Liechtenstein has won more Olympic medals per capita than any other nation.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> It is the smallest nation to win a medal in any Olympics, Winter or Summer, and currently the only nation to win a medal in the Winter Games but not in the Summer Games. Other notable skiers from Liechtenstein are <a href="/wiki/Marco_B%C3%BCchel" title="Marco Büchel">Marco Büchel</a>, <a href="/wiki/Willi_Frommelt" title="Willi Frommelt">Willi Frommelt</a>, <a href="/wiki/Paul_Frommelt" title="Paul Frommelt">Paul Frommelt</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ursula_Konzett" title="Ursula Konzett">Ursula Konzett</a>.
</p><p>Another discipline unusually popular with Liechtensteiners is <a href="/wiki/Auto_racing" title="Auto racing">motorsport</a>; American-born German-Colombian <a href="/wiki/Rikky_von_Opel" title="Rikky von Opel">Rikky von Opel</a> raced under the flag of Liechtenstein in <a href="/wiki/Formula_One" title="Formula One">Formula One</a> in <a href="/wiki/1973_Formula_One_season" title="1973 Formula One season">1973</a> and <a href="/wiki/1974_Formula_One_season" title="1974 Formula One season">1974</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Manfred_Schurti" title="Manfred Schurti">Manfred Schurti</a> competed in 9 editions of the <a href="/wiki/24_Hours_of_Le_Mans" title="24 Hours of Le Mans">24 Hours of Le Mans</a> as a <a href="/wiki/Porsche" title="Porsche">Porsche</a> factory driver with a best finish of 4th overall in <a href="/wiki/1976_24_Hours_of_Le_Mans" title="1976 24 Hours of Le Mans">1976</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> The nation is currently represented internationally by <a href="/wiki/Fabienne_Wohlwend" title="Fabienne Wohlwend">Fabienne Wohlwend</a> and <a href="/wiki/Matthias_Kaiser" title="Matthias Kaiser">Matthias Kaiser</a> in <a href="/wiki/Endurance_racing_(motorsport)" title="Endurance racing (motorsport)">endurance racing</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Other sports Liechtenstein athletes have had success in include <a href="/wiki/Tennis" title="Tennis">tennis</a>, with <a href="/wiki/Stephanie_Vogt" title="Stephanie Vogt">Stephanie Vogt</a> and <a href="/wiki/Kathinka_von_Deichmann" title="Kathinka von Deichmann">Kathinka von Deichmann</a> both having varying degrees of success on the women's tour, as well as <a href="/wiki/Swimming_(sport)" title="Swimming (sport)">swimming</a>; both <a href="/wiki/Julia_Hassler" title="Julia Hassler">Julia Hassler</a> and <a href="/wiki/Christoph_Meier" title="Christoph Meier">Christoph Meier</a> represented the country at the <a href="/wiki/2016_Summer_Olympics" title="2016 Summer Olympics">2016 Summer Olympics</a> with the former the nations' flag bearer.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In March 2020, the distance world record for electric motorcycles was set in Liechtenstein. Artist <a href="/wiki/Michel_von_Tell" title="Michel von Tell">Michel von Tell</a> drove over 1,000 miles within 24 hours on the first electric <a href="/wiki/Harley-Davidson" title="Harley-Davidson">Harley-Davidson</a>. The Record is still current in 2023 and ended in <a href="/wiki/Ruggell" title="Ruggell">Ruggell</a>. The Event became global media attention.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Youth">Youth</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Youth">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p><a href="/wiki/Liechtenstein_national_football_team" title="Liechtenstein national football team">Liechtenstein</a> competes in the Switzerland U16 Cup Tournament, which offers young players an opportunity to play against top football clubs.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Security_and_defence">Security and defence<span class="anchor" id="Security_and_defense"></span></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Liechtenstein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Security and defence">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Liechtenstein_Police_traffic_instructor_(Verkehrsinstruktion).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/29/Liechtenstein_Police_traffic_instructor_%28Verkehrsinstruktion%29.jpg/220px-Liechtenstein_Police_traffic_instructor_%28Verkehrsinstruktion%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="178" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/29/Liechtenstein_Police_traffic_instructor_%28Verkehrsinstruktion%29.jpg/330px-Liechtenstein_Police_traffic_instructor_%28Verkehrsinstruktion%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/29/Liechtenstein_Police_traffic_instructor_%28Verkehrsinstruktion%29.jpg/440px-Liechtenstein_Police_traffic_instructor_%28Verkehrsinstruktion%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2777" data-file-height="2242" /></a><figcaption>Liechtenstein Police</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Law_enforcement_in_Liechtenstein" class="mw-redirect" title="Law enforcement in Liechtenstein">Liechtenstein National Police</a> is responsible for keeping order within the country. It consists of 87 field officers and 38 civilian staff, totaling 125 employees. All officers are equipped with <a href="/wiki/Small_arms" class="mw-redirect" title="Small arms">small arms</a>. The country has one of the world's lowest <a href="/wiki/Crime_statistics" title="Crime statistics">crime rates</a>. Liechtenstein's prison holds few, if any, inmates, and those with sentences over two years are transferred to <a href="/wiki/Judiciary_of_Austria" title="Judiciary of Austria">Austrian jurisdiction</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> The Liechtenstein National Police maintains a trilateral treaty with Austria and Switzerland that enables close cross-border cooperation among the police forces of the three countries.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Liechtenstein follows a policy of <a href="/wiki/Neutrality_(international_relations)" class="mw-redirect" title="Neutrality (international relations)">neutrality</a> and is one of the few <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_without_armed_forces" title="List of countries without armed forces">countries in the world that maintain no military</a> although its police force maintains a paramilitary force, the Princely Liechtenstein Security Corps, within the organisation that might act as its <i>de facto</i> army if an invasion of Liechtenstein ever occurred. The corps provides heavy backup for the National Police as well as Honor Guards at the Royal Palace and official functions. However, Liechtenstein can reinstate its military if deemed necessary, although this is very unlikely.<sup id="cite_ref-a_103-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-a-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The army was abolished for financial reasons soon after the <a href="/wiki/Austro-Prussian_War_of_1866" class="mw-redirect" title="Austro-Prussian War of 1866">Austro-Prussian War of 1866</a>, in which Liechtenstein fielded an army of 80 men, although they were not involved in any fighting. No casualties were incurred; in fact, the unit numbered 81 upon return due to an Italian military liaison who accompanied the army back home.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> The demise of the <a href="/wiki/German_Confederation" title="German Confederation">German Confederation</a> in that war freed Liechtenstein from its international obligation to maintain an army, and parliament seized this opportunity and refused to provide funding for one. The Prince objected, as such a move would leave the country defenceless, but relented on 12 February 1868 and disbanded the force. The last soldier to serve under the colours of Liechtenstein died in 1939 at age 95.<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li>In 1985 the Swiss Army fired off shells during an exercise and mistakenly burned a patch of forest inside Liechtenstein. The incident was said to have been resolved "over a case of white wine".<sup id="cite_ref-Letzing_90-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Letzing-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>In March 2007, a 170-man Swiss infantry unit got lost during a training exercise and inadvertently crossed 1.5&#160;km (0.9 miles) into Liechtenstein. The accidental invasion ended when the unit realized their mistake and turned back.<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> The Swiss Army later informed Liechtenstein of the incursion and offered official apologies,<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> to which an internal ministry spokesperson responded, "No problem, these things happen."<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>On 20 September 2017, Liechtenstein signed the United Nations <a href="/wiki/Treaty_on_the_Prohibition_of_Nuclear_Weapons" title="Treaty on the Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons">Treaty on the Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>

The mountain village of Triesenberg is a modern preserve of the Walser people and their dialect.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=bookitem&amp;rft.atitle=Principality+of+Liechtenstein&amp;rft.btitle=The+Microstates+of+Europe%3A+Designer+Nations+in+a+Post-Modern+World&amp;rft.place=Lanham%2C+Maryland&amp;rft.series=Anthropology%2C+political+science&amp;rft.pages=41&amp;rft.pub=Lexington+Books&amp;rft.date=2012-11-29&amp;rft.isbn=978-0-7391-7427-2&amp;rft.aulast=Klieger&amp;rft.aufirst=P.+Christiaan&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fbooks.google.com%2Fbooks%3Fid%3DCrfwGa4aCwYC&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ALiechtenstein" class="Z3988"></span></span>
